for the greens/efa group madam president, i may be speaking to you on a subject which will surprise you as a green, but it is the subject on which manfred weber spoke to us and stéphane séjourné has just spoken to us. competitiveness. a green who talks about competitiveness. what's going on? is it because he's 60 that he starts talking about this kind of thing? no, not at all. but i'd like to get back to basics. what is competitiveness? it is the ability to offer goods or services of high value at the lowest possible price. so it's a ratio between a numerator and a denominator. you're gonna say i'm teaching you a lesson, but you have to remember the basics. the cost is the denominator. do you seriously think that europe can or wants to be the champion of low cost? no, i'm not going. we have a social structure that we take pride in. yes, we want to pay our workers properly. we want goods and services to be produced in an environmentally friendly way. and all of this is costing extra money. so we'll never be the champion of the low cost. so the only way for the european union to be competitive is to be the champion of high value. and so the question we have to ask ourselves is, what are high-value goods and services in the 21st century? in a sense, the european union has so far failed to embrace the digital revolution. but what is the big game of the 21st century? it's about making our societies and economies compatible with the biophysical limits of the planet. this is where competitiveness in the 21st century comes into play. and so for the european union, it is not economy. you know, today the european automotive industry says that we're still not going to play the american or chinese champions of the electric car. but this is not the result of over-regulation comfort of rentier capitalism. that's what the greens are calling for. and so, contrary to what one might think, the greens are not economically backward. i think we understand the challenges of this century better than anyone else.

on behalf of the green group, madam president, commissioner, the failure of silicon valley bank and two other smaller us banks is a cause for concern, as it is the biggest failure since the great financial crisis of 2008. unfortunately, i have to say that the lessons of the 2008 crisis seem to have been forgotten. we have been warning for a long time in my group about the risk of the ongoing reversal of financial regulation. and the case of silicon valley bank gives us reason. since trump's deregulatory actions in 2018, silicon valley bank has been subject to less stringent regulatory scrutiny and requirements on the grounds of proportionality. but i regret to say that the same trend is being observed in the union, where we are moving dangerously towards a basle agreement which will not respect international standards. this crisis should also be a wake-up call to complete the banking union, as we have known for a long time that we must do. a comprehensive european deposit insurance system would increase confidence and limit the risk of bank failures. it is also striking that in the last few hours the promise to refrain from bailing out the financial sector with public money has been completely broken again. the federal deposit insurance corporation in the united states has extended deposit guarantees to all depositors and, in addition, the federal reserve board has announced that it will accept collateral at face value. in other words, private profit, loss for all. if we want to reduce banking risk in europe: common deposit insurance, strengthening our recovery and resolution framework and strict compliance with the basel framework.
on behalf of the ecr group. – madam president, commissioner, the bankruptcies of silicon valley bank, silvergate and signature bank were accidents waiting to happen. two lessons should be drawn from this experience. first, central banks should stay the course and withstand pressure to deviate from their present policy trajectory, not only to fight inflation, but also to contain excessively speculative behaviour. should central banks give in to the pleas for monetary expansion, then new impulses are given to the disastrous cycle of excessive debt accumulation and asset bubbles ending always in major financial accidents. secondly, financial regulation or financial regulators always tend to fight the last war. a better way to financial stability are higher capital requirements for banks or financial institutions in general to ensure absorption of shocks and lower the probability of bailouts by taxpayers. this lesson is very relevant for the ongoing trilogues on the banking package. it is not too late to finalise the post-crisis reforms 15 years after 2008 with the faithful implementation of the basel recommendations.
madam president, madam commissioner, the scriptures tell us about the foolish virgins and the wise virgins. in the 2008 crisis the european union behaved like the foolish virgins and when the lord came they had no oil in the candle. at that time the european central bank took much longer than its american, japanese or british counterparts to lower interest rates or to buy assets in the markets. national governments were forced to come to the rescue of their financial institutions in complete isolation, until well into the crisis, because there was no solidarity reaction. the result was some really remarkable austerity programmes and a torpedo on the european project's waterline. did we do something right? we did something right. we have created the single supervisory mechanism for the supervision and regulation of banking, insurance, stock markets and the european systemic risk board. but we still have a few things to consider: the completion of the capital market, the european fund insurance and the crisis management mechanisms. and a word of caution: i am fully aware that the central bank's main objective is to maintain price stability, but without forgetting the other objectives of the treaty, without forgetting that it must act in such a way as not to jeopardise growth, which is very weak and may lead to recession. the interest rate policy of the central bank is independent and must be guided by these two objectives.
madam president, thank you commissioner for being with us for this important debate. the failure of silicon valley bank teaches us some lessons. commissioner has already outlined several of them, but i want to stress two of them in particular. the first is that an ambitious prudential regulation is a necessary condition not only for having healthy and solid banks, but also for preventing financial dominance. the second lesson is that either there is a fully fledged bank crisis management framework able to deal with both idiosyncratic and systemic crises or, in the end, only citizens pay the bailout bill. in the eu, we were pretty good on the first point – although the work has yet to be completed and i agree with most of the things that our rapporteur fernández said on the basel iii negotiations – but momentum was lost on the second part, the bank crisis management framework. if we really want to strengthen the banking system and make economic policy truly independent, we have to deliver as soon as possible on one thing: the completion of the banking union.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, given that the present us banking crisis is very different from previous ones, we cannot help but emphasize these considerations. first of all: the failure of silicon valley bank was triggered by the default of a financial and banking platform linked to crypto assets. are we sure that the european banking system is free from the same risk? secondly: the monetary policy undertaken by the ecb in the last year on raising rates to counter rising inflation has exposed the financial banking system to systemic risks such as those of 2008 in the extreme: the sudden rise in interest rates was matched by a fall in the prices of bonds in circulation, creating potential liquidity problems in the banking system. it could manifest what economists call the "black swan theory". these considerations should prompt the ecb, as is already happening with the fed, to immediately revise its outlook on interest rate dynamics, along with more reassuring communication to citizens and businesses, already hugely affected by the recent rise in the cost of borrowing and lending.

mr president, commissioner, minister, ladies and gentlemen, for the umpteenth time we are standing before this house with the terrible images of a tragedy like that of cutro still before our eyes: the broken lives of children, women, men who had placed their hopes and dreams of freedom in europe. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the situation in the baltic sea is not affected by the recent events. i look into the eyes of those who have decision-making responsibilities. i look the italian government in the eye and see that it has not yet been able to give any answers. and it is unbearable that a minister should start a syndicate about why a mother and father choose to entrust the lives of their children to such a dangerous journey. we know them, as we know the answers this house must give, from legal immigration channels to binding redistribution mechanisms. ladies and gentlemen, let us not simply say, once again, what we are waiting for, because otherwise the answer the next day will always be the same: let's wait until it's too late!
mr president, dear colleagues, the shipwrecks on the mediterranean are heart-breaking human tragedies. blaming the legitimate border protection efforts of member states, however, is a very dishonest and a completely wrong approach. the underlying reason for these tragic events, in reality, is the european indulgence and lack of sanction concerning illegal entries into the union. it is the pro-migration statements, the quotas and the impunity for ngos that cooperate with criminals – smugglers – that cause these deaths. italy faces the continuous arrival of more and more ships, with an estimated 650 000 people set to leave africa just this year, as forecast by intelligence services. it is european migration policy that must be placed on a completely new footing in order to avoid more human tragedies at sea. to stop the vicious circle, the first thing to do is to start protecting europe’s borders so that there would be no point for smugglers and migrants to even begin their dangerous journey. also, all illegally arrived migrants in europe who have no right to stay must be transported back home. unless we do these things, there will be no end to the tragedies that we have witnessed.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the images of the bodies on the beach of cutro and those floating off the coast of libya do not give me peace. my mind went back to ten years ago, when in lampedusa i was among the first to rescue those 500 people and to inspect 368 bodies. what has happened and what is still happening over time is the manifestation of a europe that is unable to cope with the phenomenon of migration. it's a disgrace! this is outrageous! we can no longer tolerate politics and bureaucratic backlashes on people's lives. a european search and rescue mission is also needed to restore humanity to politics. i want you all to imagine for a moment that those children returned from the sea are your children, your grandchildren, our children, our grandchildren. so what? then let 's start working together: parliament, council and commission, in the knowledge that everything can be negotiated: but not the core values, not life.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, italy, the marvellous italian seas, are becoming cemeteries for thousands of human beings, women and children. cutro's grief has overwhelmed us. and the italian government has failed to save the lives of children, women and men and has dared to say, with minister piantedosi, that it is the fault of those who leave with their children. it's a slap in the face to centuries of civilization. human lives must always be saved and those fleeing persecution and war must be welcomed. this horror fills us with shame, not only italians, but all european citizens. i was elected four years ago, and from day one i called for a revision of the dublin treaties, which place the responsibility for dealing with a migration phenomenon of epochal size and impact on one country, and which affects all european countries. 26,000 dead since the lampedusa massacre to date. you know what i mean? in the face of a naive, unprepared and clumsy italian government, at least europe should realize that this tragic reality can only be faced by each of us taking our own responsibility: investing people, resources and money to save lives today and create the conditions to cut off the business of human beings tomorrow. europe must make a choice of dignity and humanity and respect its roots. if it doesn't, our seas will still be water graveyards.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, you are hypocrites. the dead at sea are the sole responsibility of the european union. bonhomie and pro-immigration policies have led to more departures and therefore more deaths at sea. your pathetic slogans for welcoming people are a disservice to migrants and to the citizens of the european union. it's embarrassing your attitude to immigration: on the one hand, you talk about humanity, then leave italy alone, with all the responsibility of the reception. you gave 7 billion euros to erdogan. yet boats continue to leave turkey. you talk about european solidarity, but the numbers on relocations are practically ridiculous, there is no rotation of european ports and nobody wants economic migrants. enough of your solidarity propaganda! we must stop illegal immigration and seriously combat the pirates. and then, you know, with all this rhetoric and all your hypocrisy, with all the deaths at sea: how do you look in the mirror in the morning?
in the name of the id group. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, two months ago one of the most dangerous bosses of the italian mafia, matteo messina denaro, was arrested. an important achievement that needs to be dedicated to the memory of law enforcement officers who lost their lives fighting crime. two of them, falcone and borsellino, were martyrs of the mafia massacres. today, organized crime is increasingly diversified and uses new technologies. but as always, he's doing business by exploiting the weakest: vulnerable and blackmailed immigrants are the ones most often used as labour in european cities. the crime first asks for 8 000 or 9 000 euros to get to europe and then continues its chain of exploitation. many of them end up doing low-wage, no-contract work and in the worst-case scenario, end up on our streets begging, stealing, dealing or prostitution. that is why the italian government recently decided to increase the penalties for scuba divers to 30 years in prison. those who work on the skin of the weakest do not deserve leniency. to fight crime we must have faith in the law and especially in those who are deputized to enforce it. stop criminalizing our police force, as the left has done in recent years. our officers need support and resources.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, if we are to combat organized crime, we must remember that it is vital for these organizations to have support and cover in the economic, financial, political and institutional world. we can cut off the visible branches by locking up the bosses and the manoeuvring: it's only fair and proper. but the real challenge is to uproot these organizations: the commission has already taken steps to ensure that the european parliament is able to exercise its right to vote and to stand as a candidate in the elections to the european parliament. in addition, european countries prefer to ignore certain criminal organizations, such as those involved in the trafficking of waste to the poorest countries, a trafficking closely linked to arms trafficking, often covered up by international cooperation or by associations pretending to have humanitarian aims. one last word about the mafia: the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat this problem.
in the name of the id group. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, what is discrimination? the dictionary says it's a distinction made as a result of a judgment or a classification. and do you know what the paradox of this directive against discrimination is? the paradox is that this directive has too many classifications but not enough. for example, you had to add the plus to the lgbtq acronym because you ran out of letters in the alphabet to classify diversity. but the world is made up of diversity, of individuals, each different from the other. and how are you going to rank all of them in your charts? inevitably, some will be left out and discriminated against. what do most of the world's constitutions say? that all men are the same? let us return to this shared and sacred principle and try to give everyone equal opportunity, which does not mean ad hoc laws for every individual in the world. rules, to work, must be general and abstract. the member states have blocked this directive because the problems of implementation are real and those real problems are the ones that citizens face every day and that policy should deal with more.
in the name of the id group. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the atrocities of the islamic regime in iran are becoming more and more chilling. hundreds of girls have been poisoned in numerous schools across iran. it was mass poisoning. someone wants to deny iranian women their right to education. it is a form of revenge against the many female students who participated in the protests and made videos posted on social media where they remove their headscarves and shout anti-government slogans. we also saw five girls doing an islamic veilless dance in the middle of the street: well, those five girls were arrested and forced to apologize with their heads covered. the prohibitions imposed by the islamic dictatorship fully represent their conception of women: with the islamic veil, submissive, uneducated, an inferior being. we must support the girls who are fighting and condemn the acts of terror carried out by the regime against women's freedom. just be quiet, just close your eyes. - what? life. freedom is free.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, it is important that european citizens are in some way forcing parliament to discuss how to safeguard bees, creatures which are fundamental to the role of pollination and therefore to human life and the biological balance. the value of what bees do worldwide can be estimated at about 153 billion euros a year, about 15 billion in europe alone. we therefore call, without further ado, for at least four immediate actions: funds and a programme for the rational restoration of a wildflower habitat in europe; redirect cap payments to support bee pollination services and re-population where bees have become drastically rare; reform the regulation of pesticides to prevent future harm to bees; to improve the biosecurity of soil and potted plants currently on the market. we must all remember that with the suffering of these creatures, the entire biological heritage and the entire economy linked to it is at risk. so, not acting would be unconscious.
in the name of the id group. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, on 10 april 1998 i was a young activist and a passionate follower of the irish question. the good friday agreement has taught me a lesson that i carry with me, and that is that it is also necessary to try to understand the other person's reasons when dealing with a political issue. in this case, then, we are talking about an agreement which has served to end decades of bloodshed on our continent. that is why, among the appeals that have already come in the last few days, i emphasize that of the christian churches, to all of us to try to live up to the standards of those who have gone before us, the men who made our continent great, to achieve the goals of peace, prosperity and freedom in all its dimensions.

the next item is the debate on the council and commission statements on the conclusions of the european council meeting of 23-24 march 2023 (2023/2580(rsp)).
president of the european council. madam president of the parliament, madam president of the commission, ladies and gentlemen of the house. ladies and gentlemen, the world is more dangerous and predictable today. the multilateral rules-based order is under threat and this is precisely why we are strengthening our eu-united nations cooperation. and it is why secretary-general guterres joined our european council meeting last week. our discussions focused on three topics: the war in ukraine, climate change and reform in the global financial system. russia is attacking ukraine and attacking our international rules-based system but the multilateral system is resisting and standing firm. the un has played a key role in finding concrete solutions on humanitarian support to refugees, on the black sea grain initiative and on nuclear facilities. the eu and united nations also cooperate on many global issues such as climate change and, for example, we have seen recently with the historic agreement on the high seas treaty. the 27 eu leaders also reaffirmed their commitment to the 2030 agenda and its sustainable development goals, and re-discussed the importance of reforming the global financial system to fight poverty and to make the world fairer. ladies and gentlemen, president zelenskyy has addressed every european council since the start of the war, and last week was no exception. and president zelenskyy made one thing very clear: to defend itself, ukraine needs more weapons and more ammunition. we endorsed the decision on ammunition agreed by the foreign affairs council. we acted quickly. it took barely six weeks after prime minister kallas proposed the idea and when we tasked the high representative with taking it forward. our goal is to provide 1 million rounds of ammunition within the next 12 months. and this joint eu initiative constitutes a major new step towards a genuine european defence. we also discussed the recent international criminal court's decision. this is the beginning of a process to hold russian leaders accountable for their crimes in ukraine. and we also agreed to step up efforts, along with our partners, to prevent the circumvention and backfilling of our sanctions. ladies and gentlemen, yesterday i was in moldova, where we shared the eu's strong message of solidarity with the moldovans. we also prepared the 2nd meeting of the european political community, which will take place in chișinău on 1 june. moldova is under growing pressure from russia. it needs our support. we have mobilised over eur 1 billion in the past year to support its resilience and stability and we are ready to do more. that's why we've invited the commission to present a support package for moldova before summer. ladies and gentlemen, dear colleagues, we also had an important debate on our long-term competitiveness, and we agreed to focus on three areas. first, building smarter regulation. we need to make life easier for our businesses and create the conditions for our businesses to flourish right here in the eu. and this means cutting red tape, faster permitting, shorter deadlines, easier procedures and digital solutions. second, unlocking investment to ensure our long-term competitiveness. we need massive investments in technology, innovation, connectivity. financing is the lifeblood of our european businesses, especially our smes, so we need to make it easier for them to get the private capital and investment they need to innovate, scale up and thrive. and we have the financial means at our disposal. savings represent eur 1.5 trillion in the european union. the capital markets union will help make this happen. it's time to speed up work and get this done. and third, boosting research and innovation. the eu needs to become a powerhouse of innovation, especially in areas with tremendous growth potential like renewable and cleantech. we will therefore increase investment in r&d, help innovative products and services get on the markets. we also discussed trade essential to our prosperity. since the 1950s, trade has grown from 5 percent to 25 percent of our gdp, and we need to consider a number of issues. trade is influenced by geopolitics, and we must navigate our trade relations in this complex landscape. in relation to the us, we are a close and strategic ally. in recent months, we have discussed our economic cooperation, notably on the iia and the wto reform. we are working together with the united states to find a solution to the issues raised by the ira. as for china, we face the reality as it is today. china is a major trading partner and has become more assertive and more challenging. so we need to be clear on our goal. we want to derisk, not to the decouple. it is also important to maintain a multilateral trade system with a reformed world trade organization at its core, and this includes its dispute settlement mechanism. the 27 leaders agreed to continue our ambitious trade agenda while defending our interests. on migration, we continued our work from last month's summit. we are implementing the concrete measures agreed, and the council presidency and the commission have updated us on the progress so far. we will continue our work on this. it's a priority for all of us. dear colleagues, we also held on friday a euro summit with the participation of christine lagarde and paschal donohoe. and this meeting focused on two key points. first, the current economic and financial situation. our economies entered 2023 on a healthier footing than expected but the impact on inflation remains a serious concern, and we agreed to continue to closely coordinate our economic policy responses and we also reiterated that the economic governance framework is key for a strong economic and monetary union. second, we focused on our european financial architecture. our banking sector is resilient, with strong capital and liquidity positions, and the banking union has significantly strengthened our resilience. but we shouldn't be complacent. we therefore called for continued efforts to complete our banking union. speeding up progress on the capital markets union is also a top priority. the eu is a staunch defender of multilateralism, and the rules-based international order will continue to work to make europe stronger and more sovereign, and to make the world a fairer place for everyone.
president of the commission. – president metsola, president michel, honourable members. indeed, we had a lot of topics on the agenda in the last european council. i want to focus on three of them, and the centrepiece was certainly competitiveness with the view of our response to the inflation reduction act. you are very familiar with the backdrop, so we discussed how to answer to the inflation reduction act. you know what's on the table. the first part is the negotiated part. you know that we confirmed with our american friends our understanding that european carmakers get access to the us market and to us tax breaks with leased electric vehicles. the second part is that we negotiate an agreement that will ensure that critical raw minerals for batteries that are extracted and processed here in the european union are treated equally to those from canada and mexico, for example, as if we had a free trade agreement. third, president biden and i established a transparency dialogue on the clean tech subsidies. this is one part; this is the negotiated part. but the bigger and more important part is that we have to do our homework here in the european union. we know that, today, european companies are leaders in net—zero research and innovation. we have set ourselves very ambitious targets for the deployment of renewables. however, our policies have a gap, and that is the support for the manufacturing of clean technologies. you all know this situation where, the moment a new technology moves to commercial deployment at scale, and so is really scaling up, that is just the moment where manufacturing often moves outside the european union. we know that china is the largest producer of all mass—manufactured clean tech, from batteries to solar panels, from certain types of electrolysers to heat pumps. this is especially concerning because, according to the international energy agency, renewables will account for over 90% of global electricity expansion in the next five years. in other words, china will be the biggest winner of this expansion, and if we do not act right now, this chinese dominance over the clean tech value chain will become even more – not less – pronounced. this is why the commission has put forward two acts. the first is the net—zero industry act and the second is the critical raw materials act. we discussed the net—zero industry act. it will create a regulatory environment that is conducive to scaling up the clean tech industry in europe, so precisely the gap that we have right now, in order to fill this gap and to really encourage scaling—up. this is combined with european funding or state aid – both possibilities are possible – and with a matching clause, so that we have transparency on what happens on the american market. in other words, the net—zero industry act aims at keeping european companies in europe, and not seeing them emigrate across the atlantic or to china. the second element we discussed is the proposal of the critical raw materials act on the table. now, we all know that the critical raw materials are vital for our twin transition, namely decarbonisation and digitalisation. while the demand for these raw materials is projected to increase drastically, we know that europe heavily depends on imports. of course, this dependency that we have on imports threatens not only our climate and digital objectives, but also weakens our industrial base. therefore, the critical raw materials act aims at significantly improving the conditions for extracting, processing and – very importantly – recycling these critical raw materials here in the european union. at the same time, we are strengthening our cooperation with reliable partners around the world, whether it be zinc or nickel from canada, or lithium from chile. mesdames et messieurs les députés, mon deuxième thème: l’ukraine. l’ukraine a été un autre sujet important de nos discussions la semaine dernière. depuis le tout premier jour de la guerre, nous avons travaillé ensemble dans un seul but: faire échouer le plan de poutine visant à effacer l’ukraine de la carte. pour atteindre ce but, nous avons intensifié notre aide financière. nous avons mis sur pied une plateforme de reconstruction pour rebâtir ce beau et héroïque pays. enfin, et c’est une vraie première pour notre union, nous avons acheté en commun des armes dont l’ukraine a besoin pour se défendre. notre prochaine étape sera de doter l’ukraine des munitions dont elle a grandement besoin. nous allons donc investir 2 milliards d’euros venant de la facilité européenne pour la paix, pour assurer la livraison continue de munitions. elles viendront de stocks existants ou feront l’objet d’une acquisition conjointe. nous devons aussi aider notre industrie de la défense à augmenter sa capacité de production de munitions, y compris avec l’appui budgétaire de l’union européenne. c’est-à-dire qu’il faut agrandir les installations de production, renforcer les chaînes d’approvisionnement et soutenir la réaffectation de sites de fabrication existants. nous avons dit que nous soutiendrons l’ukraine quoi qu’il en coûte, et l’europe tiendra sa promesse. und das dritte thema, über das ich aus dem europäischen rat berichten möchte, ist ein anderer aspekt des russischen kriegs in der ukraine. jeder krieg ist grausam. aber dieser krieg zeigt eine grausamkeit und brutalität des kremls, was eine ganz neue dimension entwickelt. ich spreche zum beispiel von der verschleppung ukrainischer kinder. putins schergen sollen rund 16 200 kinder nach russland entführt haben. sie wurden aus ihren familien und aus ihrer kindheit gerissen. sie wurden ihrer heimat und ihrer gewohnten umgebung beraubt und, wenn wir nicht aktiv werden, auch ihrer zukunft. das zynische adoptionsgesetz, das russland im vergangenen mai erlassen hat, erleichtert die adoption dieser kinder aus der ukraine. das risiko ist also groß, dass sie für immer im nirgendwo verschwinden. das sind kriegsverbrechen! die, die daran beteiligt sind, müssen zur verantwortung gezogen werden! genau aus diesem grund hat der internationale strafgerichtshof einen haftbefehl gegen den russischen präsidenten erlassen. das ist ein schritt, der seinesgleichen sucht. mit dieser entscheidung ist die welt für putin ein ganzes stück kleiner geworden. wenn er in eines der 123 länder reist, die mit dem internationalen strafgerichtshof zusammenarbeiten, riskiert er seine verhaftung. jeder besuch in einem dieser länder, sei es in afrika oder asien, sei es in europa oder südamerika, wird so für ihn zu einem unkalkulierbaren risiko. wir unterstützen den gerichtshof, bis der gerechtigkeit genüge getan ist. wir haben bereits sanktionen gegen 16 personen verhängt, die für die verschleppungen verantwortlich sind, darunter die sogenannte russische beauftragte für kinderrechte. aber wir müssen mehr tun. wir müssen versuchen, herauszufinden, wo sich die entführten kinder aufhalten. und wir for the greens/efa group madam president, mr president, first of all, thank you for being here, since you were here not long ago before the council, and i will see you again afterwards. i think that's a good formula. the last time i spoke to you about a subject on which the greens' voice may sometimes seem incongruous, it was competitiveness. today, another council theme is international trade. you're going to say, "what the hell is he talking about, international trade?" my starting question is whether europe can be self-sufficient. the answer we are asking is that even if she wanted to, she couldn't. in fact, i think we should not want to, because europe is not an island. it's part of this planet, and, if only for climate change, we know that we are dependent on each other. what we have to avoid - and recent years have reminded us of this - is over-reliance, both on the part of our suppliers - and we have seen this from russia - and from our customers; we've seen it on the chinese side as well. what i believe the european union's trade policy should aim at is interdependence at the right level and with the partners of our choice. these interdependencies, it seems to me, must have three objectives. the first is the resilience of our societies through diversification, both of suppliers and of markets. secondly - and i do not see a hierarchy in the objectives - it must be said that this is the consolidation of strategic alliances. i repeat, europe is not an island. when we also see the decline of democracy on the planet, i really think that between european and non-european democracies, we really have an interest in getting together. finally, and this is new - climate change and planetary boundaries are forcing us to do this - it's regulatory convergence. i say convergence: it does not mean instantaneous regulatory identity, but regulatory convergence, particularly in social and environmental matters. international trade cannot lead to a race to the bottom in these matters. the problem we have -- and this is where we probably have a nuance with the enthusiastic speeches, including malik's a moment ago, or manfred's a moment before -- is that all the negotiating mandates for the treaties that are being discussed have been decided, have been adopted in the world before: the world before the pandemic, the world before climate awareness, the world before the war. and so we really have to ask ourselves the question of how far we need to revise the agreements - and mercosur is a good example - to bring them into line with the strategic, climate, social and environmental objectives of the european union. that is what i am calling on us to work towards. and perhaps it is not yet a scoop that the free trade agreement with new zealand is pointing the way.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to draw your attention to the strait bridge, which is central in many ways. the european parliament has already expressed its views on the proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens at work. as minister salvini says, the strait bridge will be much more than a technologically advanced bridge, it will be a human artifact that more than any other infrastructure will symbolize the connectivity of the 21st century. the bridge over the straits is an expression of the exemplary will of italy's cohesion policy, the european and euro-mediterranean policies, all of which are aimed at improving the internal market, including the establishment of the union of defence and military mobility. europe knows that it has in sicily the largest and most important strategic base in the mediterranean on its sea, under its sea and above its sky.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the commission has proposed that cattle farming be included in the scope of the directive on industrial emissions. to say that farms pollute as much as factories is madness. over the period 1990-2020, italy has reduced its emissions by about a quarter, while morocco and turkey have increased their emissions by 23% and india by 21%. the inclusion of the beef and veal sector in this directive, with a too low number threshold, would affect most farms, encouraging the instrumental splitting of medium-sized and small farms to avoid obligations or even causing them to close, with the consequent loss of jobs. the increase in imports of meat from third countries which do not respect our food safety is inevitable. the european union will protect and defend our livestock. for the european citizens there is only one kind of meat, natural meat, from animals, not laboratory-produced meat.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, once again we are in this house discussing one of the most unpleasant forms of discrimination in existence: the gender pay gap for equal work of equal value. the principle of equal distribution must be enforced through wage transparency and enforcement mechanisms. but it is also important to note that if european companies with more than 100 employees are required to disclose wage information to expose existing gender pay gaps, this will not be the case for those with up to 100 employees, who will have no obligation and therefore no additional administrative or financial burden. the realities of small and mediumsized enterprises in italy, which are the basic fabric of our economy, must not be penalized in any way. this is the way forward to eradicate once and for all a cancer that has been poisoning the world of work for too long.
in writing. ladies and gentlemen, the principle of equal pay for men and women should not even be discussed in this house, as it should be a long-established right. unfortunately, that is not the case. the data show a 13% pay gap between men and women in europe, figures that still give us pause to reflect on the status of women in 2023. the causes of this gap are to be found in women's family choices, the lower presence of women in top positions or the presence of more women in low-paid jobs. it is time to say no more to the penalisation of women workers, who must enjoy the same rights, the same pay and the same career opportunities as men. that is why this directive is going in the right direction, striving to close the gender pay gap without imposing heavy economic or bureaucratic burdens on small and mediumsized enterprises.
in the name of the id group. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the objective of this regulation is multifaceted: the commission will continue to monitor the implementation of the eu's environmental legislation and to monitor the implementation of the eu's environmental legislation. the new rules require that imports into europe of a range of raw materials and products, including soybeans, beef, coffee, cocoa and palm oil, be accompanied by a certificate that they have not contributed to the destruction of forests. the eu's new forestry policy will be based on a strengthened traceability system and controls by member states, which will be able to suspend the placing on the eu market of products deemed to be causing deforestation. these initiatives will have to be followed by other measures which may affect imports into the european union in order to better guarantee food and environmental security and social guarantees. in this way, with the principle of reciprocity, we will be able to protect both the planet and the italian and european consumers.
mr president, drought, extreme weather events and energy dependence on non-european countries are showing us that the energy transition can no longer be postponed. it is clear that the social and environmental costs of the delays accumulated over the years are very high, and that they are being passed on to the weakest members of our society, fuelling poverty and inequality. today, 55 million european citizens, more than one in ten, are unable to pay their bills. this shows that the energy transition is doomed to fail if we do not approach it with ambition and pragmatism. the social climate fund is therefore needed for those households that do not have the means to make their homes more energy sustainable, but also for small businesses that are unable to reduce emissions in road transport and construction and therefore need to be accompanied by targeted policies and instruments. while the right wing is waging anachronistic battles and proposing spot subsidies, we socialists have found solutions and resources to enable everyone to bear the costs of the transition economically. this is how we think about the present and the future of our companies and our citizens, so that we leave no one behind.
mr president, on 11 april the italian government declared a national emergency on migrants, a decision prompted by the exceptional increase in arrivals on italian coasts, which has seen over 32 000 landings since the beginning of the year, four times more than in 2022. italy is doing its part in managing the migration phenomenon, where is the european union and what has it done? he certainly failed: we remember the disastrous deal signed in 2016 with turkey to stop the flow of migrants, a pact costing over 10 billion that has led to no results. on the migration emergency, italy does not need solidarity in words, but rapid and concrete action by a europe which must take responsibility for a european problem. if the european union exists, it is time to prove it. on the contrary, we shall see yet another failure of a common project which no longer has a reason to exist.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, covid and war have exacerbated problems that threaten to overwhelm humanity and the planet itself. i'm talking about the dramatic increase in inequality and therefore poverty and the climate and environmental emergency. article 25 of the eu directive, the corporate sustainability due diligence directive, states the obligation for companies with more than 250 employees to assess environmental and social sustainability in the short, medium and long term. it is the official introduction of the concept of "benefit society", already recognized in italy since 2015 and requested at european level by a petition that collected over 475 000 signatures. benefit societies must be the future and it must become economically and image-wise cost-effective for every business to be 100 per cent sustainable. this parliament, and in particular the juri committee, is committed to upholding article 25 without distorting it. the future, let us always remember, is ours to build.

in the name of the id group. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, madam commissioner, in the past few months, higher than average temperatures have enabled us to reduce gas consumption. this is good news. this has led to lower prices and thus to an acceptable level of energy security, thanks to high levels of gas in storage. but we must not view the challenge as near its happy conclusion. we must not be under the illusion that it is enough to keep the tanks full to avoid the danger of running out of fuel for the next winter, since that would be a failure of planning. energy storage, important as it is, cannot replace the imports that are lacking. there has been justified talk of strengthening cooperation agreements with suppliers such as algeria and azerbaijan, or of resuming important projects such as dismed, while imports of liquefied natural gas have increased very considerably, which certainly helped to avert a crisis in the winter that has just ended. a thought: the increase in lng imports in 2022 was made possible by a particular situation, with a decrease in demand from china, from south east asian markets, although the international energy agency warned that a return of purchases by china to significant levels such as those in 2021 could cause europe to face a fuel shortage, with a gap between demand and supply of 27 billion cubic meters. that's a lot. so it is right to continue the commitment to decarbonisation. but europeans, our people, are asking for reassurance, because we cannot pray that a mild winter will make up for the lack of planning.
madam president, the industrial and geopolitical future of europe is mainly in the field of energy. yet, so far, europe has not been able to respond adequately to the challenges posed by the russian-ukrainian conflict. for years it has demonized gas, a resource which has proved to be strategic and will remain so for many years to come, it has struck energy agreements with unstable and unreliable countries such as azerbaijan, and above all it has not backed down a millimeter in its mad rush towards the electric transition. the very high cost of these ideological choices, already paid for by households and businesses, is set to increase even more with the end of the endothermic engines from 2035. a self-destructive measure that will further raise prices, diminish the purchasing power of the citizens and put our manufacturing in crisis. we will certainly listen to the commission's proposals for next winter, but let us make it clear from the outset that we will not be advancing naïve and disastrous green solutions. the commission has not yet taken a decision on the application of the exemption from the obligation to provide information to the public.
madam president, commissioners, the improvement of the cybersecurity of digital products is a step forward in protecting users and businesses from the consequences of cyberattacks which have become increasingly frequent and targeted. security should not be seen as a cost but as an investment, as every data breach has an economic impact that could be avoided through the adoption of advanced technologies. i would like to draw attention to one critical point about the new regulation. in fact, there is a risk of blocking the development of open source in europe. according to article 16 of the cyber resilience act, programmers could be held responsible for security breaches in their software even if they receive no financial return, and this could damage the innovative drive of open source software which, as we recall, plays a prominent role in the european digital economy. we are talking about a hundred billion impact, so it is essential that any legislation takes into account the unique prospects of open source software and that future regulations must also take into account the difficulties of small and medium-sized enterprises in finding effective solutions to protect them. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. we are not, because we are talking about realities that often do not have the resources to adapt in such a short time. an example of this is italy, where three out of four companies - we are always talking about small and medium-sized enterprises - do not have a disaster recovery plan, and this could cause damage to companies, which are not only unprotected but also at risk of being mocked by sanctions. i would like to conclude, ladies and gentlemen, by calling for the attention of the world of small and mediumsized enterprises, since we are here to help them and not to invent new obstacles.

member of the commission. mr president, i will be very brief in my reply. when we talk about the erasmus+ education programme, it is clear that we have fundamental values and principles which are clearly defined in article 2 of the treaty. with the erasmus+ programme, we ensure that they are respected. whether there are attempts or not, we are there so that we can follow up and see what happens. and we have never hesitated to react if that were the case. now, i think we need to make sure that we also focus on the positive things, give them more visibility and encourage more people to see the opportunities that are out there. this is the best way, when you see that there is an example, an opportunity that is concrete for you, not to be misled by other discourses, but on the contrary, by the power of the example, to be able to ensure that you, you are the one who can benefit from this and realize projects, give shape to your ideas, thanks to this participation. i am making sure that erasmus+ does just that. this parliament fought for the erasmus+ programme budget to be doubled. we have 28 billion euros. i don't see it as a sum of money. i see this as millions of european citizens, young people with or without experience, who can participate. and that is exactly what we must continue to do.

member of the commission. thank you very much for asking. indeed, discovereu is no longer a pilot project, it is an integral part of the erasmus+ programme. and it is indeed a very successful program. remember, because of covid, last year we were able to distribute 70 train tickets. this year, it continues. indeed, what's new in discovereu is that we can include cultural routes, to see that discovereu is not just about traveling, it's also about knowing another culture, it's also about making friends. it also means visiting places that you may not have the opportunity to visit as spontaneously. so today, discovereu remains a true example of how, at european level, we can encourage our young people to be open, to know the culture and the different places in europe and thus also come out stronger in the belief and in their european identity. our analyses clearly show that everyone who participated, whether in discovereu or erasmus+, left with this sense of european belonging. i would also like to mention the need to promote, perhaps even better, another major innovation in the erasmus+ programme, namely the jean monnet actions. there is something new now with the jean monnet programme: we can also support classes that can exchange to learn more about the functioning of the european union, european integration. we can also support teachers. it seems to me that now, perhaps, we need to make some extra effort because there are new possibilities that are being offered. let's be very honest here. getting to know the european union, how the institutions work, is a journey that is not an end in itself. but now with the new erasmus+ programme, we are offering many more opportunities for teachers, students, whole classes to benefit from this experience and to learn more.

member of the commission. i will give the example of a direct contribution to a request made by young people for the european year of skills although with the parliament it has not yet started, it will be in may. just today, the college adopted two recommendations to the council on digital skills. it's a fundamental question for our students. and i'm talking about numbers, for example, in terms of schools getting access to a fast enough connection. today, less than 20 percent of our students who are in schools have a connection that goes beyond 100 megabits. similarly, one third of our students do not have the basic digital skills. we are a long way from the ambitions we set for 2030. here is a very concrete example for me of a topic that was one of the priorities of the european year of youth and that makes a direct contribution to the european year of skills. the same recommendations also refer to the digital divide, which has been the subject of much discussion in this house. yes, we must continue to reduce it. it is clear that our member states, for the first time, must have an inclusive strategy that allows them to address the issue of equipment, the issues of connectivity, the issue of skills, the issue of teachers. these are examples of topics that i think we discussed during the european year of youth and which are now a direct contribution to the european year of skills. what i could say is that i will ensure that all the priorities of the european year of youth, with the solutions that have been largely supported by young people in these different areas, are reflected in the european year of skills and above all are recognised as a contribution of this youth that made the european year of youth not a success but a vision and a key message is that the european year of youth must never stop.

the next item is the debate on the statements by the council and the commission on the need for european solidarity in saving lives in the mediterranean, particularly in italy (2023/2656 ((rsp)).
president-in-office of the council. – mr president, honourable members, commissioner, according to the international organization for migration, more than 400 migrants died in the central mediterranean in the first three months of 2023. we have not seen any deadlier quarter there in this past six years. i’m deeply saddened by the continued loss of lives by those many migrants, including parents with children. this is an unsustainable and unacceptable situation, first and foremost, from a humanitarian perspective. as i stated before, in this house, preventing these tragedies is an absolute priority that requires collective political action. now is the time for all eu institutions to move forward and deliver together on the reform of our migration and asylum policies. therefore, i warmly welcome the progress in parliament and that you voted on important files on the migration pact. now, we all need to come together, the parliament and member states, to find common ground and solutions where we can at all meet. we need a functional internal system to be able to handle the external dimension on migration. the current situation in tunisia, which now affects our member state, italy, is just one example where we need to act together and where we need common tools. there is a broad agreement in the council that we need to adopt effective and lasting solutions to address the immediate and ongoing challenges on all routes, including the central mediterranean route. in this regard. the special european council in february recognised the specific nature of maritime borders, including when it comes to protecting lives. the european council conclusions also underlined the need for reinforced cooperation regarding search and rescue activities. let me now say a few words on some of the main lines of action that the eu is pursuing. we welcome the relaunch of the european contract group on search and rescue by the european commission and the commitment of several member states to set up an enhanced cooperation framework for search and rescue amongst relevant actors, including international humanitarian and maritime organisations. as you are aware, upon request of member states, the commission has developed an action plan for the central mediterranean. the action plan focused on the already mentioned coordinated approach to search and rescue, but also on enhanced cooperation with partners, countries and international organisations. it also includes the implementation of the voluntary solidarity mechanism to deal with the situation of migration, migratory pressure on the various routes and to facilitate transfer transfers of persons rescued at sea from the member states where they disembark. the eu needs a comprehensive migration policy that protects our borders, provides protection to those in need, and prevents the loss of migrants’ lives. the presidency is starting trialogue meetings on several legislative proposals on the pact on migration and asylum, and is working hard to obtain a negotiation mandate on other files of the pact. i thank you once again for the progress that has been made on the pact recently in your house. the swedish presidency will do our part to honour the agreement that was struck between the parliament and five council presidency with a view to reform eu migration and asylum policy before next year’s eu elections. thank you very much. i’m looking forward to the debate.
member of the commission. – mr president, minister, honourable members, on our agenda today, solidarity, solidarity with people, human beings who flee war or persecution because of the colour of their skin, or people who see no choice but the dangerous journey in hope of a better life. we must treat everyone with dignity and humanity, whether they face asylum or return. but solidarity also between member states – member states of destination and member states at the borders facing large numbers of arrivals. like italy this very moment, with more than 30 000 arrivals in the first three months of this year alone: almost a 300% increase compared to the same period last year. more than 1 500 arrived last weekend alone. many arrive on the island of lampedusa. the coast of lampedusa is also the coast of the european union. the people who arrive there are our shared responsibility. we support italy as a union. we have managed to relocate 950 people – two thirds from italy, many from lampedusa – thanks to the voluntary solidarity mechanism set up last june. we want member states to relocate more and faster. the commission coordinates and encourages member states to step up their efforts. we support italy with our funds. italy is the single biggest recipient of eu migration money, receiving almost eur 2 billion since 2015 from our migration and asylum funds. the commission is currently discussing with italy additional possible emergency financial support. we support italy with our eu agencies, which work closely with the italian police and coast guard, hand in hand and day by day, the eu asylum agency works with 200 staff in italy, also in lampedusa, where they help migrants communicate with authorities, and more than 300 frontex staff and six coastal patrol boats, two aeroplanes and one helicopter. they patrol the waves and search for ships in distress. last year, frontex helped save more than 50 000 lives at sea. and we support italy with our policies. we are implementing the central mediterranean action plan launched in november. as part of that plan, we continue to build strong migration partnerships with countries of origin, transit or destination. to support italy, we must strengthen our partnership with tunisia, so far, the main country of departure to italy this year. later this month, commissioner johansson will travel to tunisia to launch an anti—smuggling operational partnership to work together to prevent departures and loss of life and to increase returns, but also to provide credible alternatives for deadly journeys, launching a talent partnership with tunisia. to deal with the large increase in arrivals italy has announced a state of emergency. we understand the main objective of the state of emergency is to provide additional reception capacities with faster procedures in the current circumstances. we expect this state of emergency to help address the real challenges italy is confronted with. at the same time, no single member state can manage migration alone, and no single policy measure alone will be enough to manage migration. we need a european migration policy. we need a comprehensive migration policy and in short, we need the pact. so i thank you for taking another step forward today on the pact, another step forward towards a truly european migration policy, namely the start this morning of political negotiations between parliament and council on the asylum procedure regulation. after the start of negotiations on eurodac earlier this year and before the start, hopefully of negotiations, next week on screening, this shows europe can move forward on migration and asylum, that europe is moving forward on migration and asylum. solidarity with people in need, solidarity with member states in need: we can and must do both. and i call on you to do everything you can to reach an agreement on the pact before the end of this mandate.
on behalf of the ppe group. – mr president, in the first months of this year, more than 30 000 migrants have arrived in italy – it’s about four times the number compared to the same period last year. in the central mediterranean route as a whole, there has been an increase in arrivals of over 300%. now we, as the epp, have been warning for a long time that we are sleepwalking into a new migration crisis and these numbers prove once again that our warnings were and are justified. the only difference is we are no longer sleepwalking into a crisis; we seem to have arrived in the middle of one, and it’s time to wake up. declaring a state of emergency, like in italy, is not only to speed up procedures and to take the immense pressure off the system. it is also a cry for help – a call for solidarity, for european solidarity, for a european challenge, and not the empty promise of solidarity that we have seen from member states like france or germany, but real, tangible help. in this context, i am happy that in this house, under the leadership of our rapporteur, tomas tobé, we have agreed on a position on the new regulation that will ensure fair responsibility-sharing. it is a crucial piece of the architecture under the new pact, and even though i am happy that some negotiations have started, it is absolutely crucial that we start negotiations on this crucial piece as soon as possible as well. but we also have to be realistic in our expectations. if arrivals continue to increase at the current rate, member states will never be able to cope, regardless of european rules on solidarity and regardless of the successful adoption of the pact. if we want to be really able to manage migration, we will also need to bring the number of irregular arrivals down. the external dimension of our migration policy is key in that regard. cooperation with countries of origin and transit, on border management, on returns and readmission and on the fight against criminal smuggling gangs must be urgently and significantly intensified. and especially tunisia, which the commissioner already mentioned, which now has taken over from libya as the most frequently used point of departure to the eu, must be our first and foremost priority. and it’s good that this is on the radar of both the council and the commission, but i would really ask you to act on this and to act on this more strongly.
on behalf of the s&d group. mr president, commissioner, exploiting the overwhelming feeling of loneliness or lack of solidarity in the face of an emergency situation in the mediterranean is within the reach of any populism, any nationalism lurking on the far right. but it is a sadly wrong answer and one that leads to frustration and failure. that is meloni's decree. the problem is real, but the answer cannot be a national solution to a pan-european challenge. the answer is to build, finally, a shared responsibility and effective, credible and binding solidarity in cases of emergency and crisis situations, as the pact that has just received its negotiating mandate approved by the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs requires. it is therefore astonishing and sad that this pact has been challenged in this parliament by the ecr group, of which meloni is vice-chairman. this is an absolutely untenable contradiction, because what this pact wants is precisely to create a regime in which solidarity is compulsory and is translated into resettlement programmes after rescue and salvage operations, which finally gives a european response to a problem which is, by definition, european: shared responsibility and binding solidarity.

mr president, the 441 deaths since the beginning of the year in the central mediterranean and the 20 000 recorded since 2014 make this migratory route, the mediterranean route, the deadliest in the world. the main gateways to europe are the italian coasts, where more than 32 000 arrivals were recorded in 2023. the declaration of a state of emergency in italy for migrants made it clear that the meloni government, beyond empty slogans about naval blockades, never had concrete solutions to alleviate the migratory pressure. policies that hinder rather than facilitate relief are costing lives. while awaiting legal and secure access to the european union, effective action against the criminal networks of traffickers and effective implementation of the european principles of solidarity and fair sharing of responsibility, italy is increasingly imposing disproportionate burdens in the management of flows and in search and rescue operations. those who call for walls on the frontiers are therefore opposed to the cooperation and common european response that are needed to avoid further journeys of despair and death.
mr president, i must thank mr manfred weber and the ppe group for introducing the subject with the decision of italy and solidarity, and immediately here in the european parliament once again the left even deleted, changed, wanted to change a title to remove the concept of solidarity from italy, because obviously they cannot have solidarity for this founding member state of europe, by saving human lives. we all agree on saving human lives, so much so that we voted in favour. even president mattarella has called for more organization. why would you do that? because this is important. what has italy done? he immediately appointed a commissioner for the state of emergency. why would you do that? because it indicates a situation of greater organization. to do what? to extend the reception and integration, which is exactly what the second level, which belongs to the local authorities, must do, and here in italy too the left always instead of helping to resolve this phase, as mattarella described it as "epic", what have they done? four regions have not declared their agreement with the new commissioner, they are obviously emilia romagna, puglia, tuscany and campania, and six large cities have also said no. so we must continue to fragment and boycott an organisation which must be established throughout the whole of the country with the full solidarity of europe. and i wanted to say to mr ferrara that we are not talking about building walls, we are talking about defending the external borders, and that is an important thing, so there is no longer any schengen, so when you go in and out of schengen it means that the databases no longer exist. this is not. the protection of migration also goes beyond the external borders, it also goes beyond strengthening the external borders and surveillance. but the left doesn't understand that.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, after 30 years of deaths at sea, do we still need to stress that human lives must be saved? instead of mourning their deaths, let's save these people. what do you mean, "what"? not by rejecting them, certainly, and certainly not by preventing them from reaching europe. if we want to talk about solidarity, we should talk about a european mare nostrum, a system of search and rescue at sea which is not just the responsibility of the countries of first entry, but shared by all the member states. i am speaking to you, colleagues on the right, and i am serious. do you really think that we are in an emergency phase or are you looking for pretexts to water down the main guarantees of the asylum system? your initiatives are damaging to italy and europe as well, and your demagogy is turning into a problem what is in fact an opportunity for the people who arrive and a wealth for the countries that welcome them. walls, fences, cpr and the criminalisation of ngos are nothing but the scapegoats of prehistoric policies, as president mattarella has argued, and, i would add, inhuman ones too.
mr president, the debate on migration is always an ideological battleground. you did not agree on the title of this debate either, and the left wanted to change it because they did not want the word 'emergency' in the title. so, what's happening in italy seems normal to you? you think that in italy there is a debate about why the left does not want the repatriation centers. a regional councillor in tuscany - and i heard tonight also the president of the tuscany region, eugenio giani - has described the cpr of the camps as for jews, thus insulting the memory of the shoah and of the entire people of israel. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. but the way forward is always the same, starting from legality. it is irregular immigration that produces deaths at sea. i've never heard of an immigrant with a regular visa who takes a plane, dies on the way. that is why we must find joint solutions. even military operations to uphold the rule of law. the european credibility is at stake. help us to stop the boat people, help us to stop the migrants from leaving, help us to promote orderly immigration and you will help us to save lives in the mediterranean. we need to firmly reaffirm in europe the right of member countries to decide who can enter our homes. we must be able to accommodate those who want to work and integrate. there's no room for the others. this is a cultural, economic and social battle. europe must return to defend its roots, its values and its borders, and it can do so by helping italy, where european culture has its ancient roots. (the speaker agrees to reply to a 'blue card' speech)
the answer is "blue card". look, in italy we saw the captain, your captain carola rackete, outflank the italian coast guard and then do something that has never been done before. we have seen in italy in recent months - because you may not know why we are here discussing the emergency in italy - we have seen 31 000 migrants arrive in italy precisely because your reception policy wants more and more of them. you attacked the government because they're not getting enough? how many do you want in europe, you haven't told us, how many immigrants do you want to bring to europe? tell the citizens. more and more and more.
in the name of the id group. madam president, thank you, mr commissioner, for being here, thank you to the colleagues who were with me, both some of them on the election mission in 2021 and on the most recent visit we made as a delegation. there are so many images that i will never forget in my life. a people coming out of 40 years of war. we in the west have important responsibilities too. external influences such as those in turkey and iran are causing very negative and problematic effects. a kurdish issue still to be resolved. these are just some of the issues that give the idea of the complexity of a reality that we have to deal with. political parties that have military militias, militias that become political parties. a political and religious sectarianism that is very similar to the one that destroyed lebanon. western men and women, men, our military who train and train the iraqis and often make a sacrifice in this regard and minorities who need to be protected more, even perhaps with our interest. it is at this time that the yazidi community celebrates the beginning of the new year. i visited their temples, temples that have come back to life. the yazidi people suffered a genocide, which the german parliament in the bundestag has recognized and which i would like to see recognized here one day. i also visited christian churches that have repopulated themselves. these communities, along with the kurds, along with the iraqis, fought and helped us to free ourselves from isis. i mean, we all owe them something. and it's because of them that isis is today a residual phenomenon, almost completely defeated. the fragility of this people, their future, the future of iraq and kurdistan, concerns us and we must deal with them more. i therefore support what the commissioner has said and i agree with him completely.
in the name of the id group. madam president, i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr lópez, for the work we are doing in this debate today. we are discussing the union's rapid deployment capability, the union's tactical groups and article 44 of the treaty on european union. this is a defence capability that member states have decided to plan, as enshrined in the new european defence doctrine, the strategic compass, and sustainability, the continued availability of forces and adequate levels of readiness are required. it also calls for the completion of the union's tactical groups through so-called 'strategic enablers', for the ambition of the compass to be reflected in a strengthening of the command and control architecture and in european industry and for synergies to be sought with nato, as strengthening the union's strategic autonomy remains a shared objective with nato. moreover, the states know that they can and must take advantage of the opportunities for cooperation provided for in article 44 of the treaty. the european and atlantic objective is one: we must do more and do better together to stop the continuing mass atrocities which are creating instability in the international community and which are preventing the planning of a better development for all, as recent events have taught us, which we always treat with great respect towards the ukrainian people, and as the bitter lesson of afghanistan has taught us.
madam president, the european union and the council of europe have a longstanding and constructive relationship aimed at protecting human rights, fundamental freedoms and democracy. this strategic partnership needs to be further strengthened and real results achieved in the areas within our competence. at the same time, however, it is important that we do not dilute our cooperation by constantly trying to push through certain issues that are controversial and on which there is no agreement between member states. i therefore oppose calls for the european union to accede to the istanbul convention as a matter of urgency, even though some member states strongly oppose such a procedure. i consider the search for clear consensus on such fundamental issues to be key to our cooperation, but also to strengthening citizens' trust in the institution. let us focus our collective strength on those areas where we can mutually agree to contribute to the protection of human rights and democracy.
madam president, today i supported the report on sustainable carbon cycles, which does not introduce new legislation yet, but is in fact the european parliament's position on the european commission's intention to set up certain rules to incentivise and reward farmers who voluntarily engage in farming towards sustainable practices and the promotion of biodiversity. we highlight the opportunity for farmers and forest managers to change their business model by turning carbon storage, so-called carbon agriculture, into a source of income. the report further recognises that food production naturally remains the top priority for the agricultural sector. in our view, carbon management should be voluntary and the rewarding of the process must be scientifically demonstrable and have a positive impact on the climate and the environment. clear frameworks for the way forward must be set, of course. i support the report.
madam president, i also supported this report. for a number of reasons. carbon capture is a real technological challenge. perhaps one of the older methods that i could mention here is that if you make a very high-quality table out of wood that will last for decades, maybe even centuries, you've done a lot of work, because there's a lot of carbon trapped in that wood. we need to use all the potential of carbon capture, both in agriculture, as my colleague has spoken about, and in the field of taking it directly from the atmosphere and putting it into products like this. i think we need to support all technologies, including the ccs method, which colleagues on the left say is unsupportable for a number of ideological reasons. we must not be ideological, but truly innovative, and i think that is fully captured in this report. although i wasn't entirely thrilled with the passage of some of the amendments.
madam president, the covid-19 pandemic has shown the need for faster digitisation of public services. digitalisation, when implemented effectively, can significantly reduce bureaucratic barriers for citizens and businesses, speed up many operations or reduce costs. i welcome the principle of 'only grow', which means that citizens do not have to report facts or provide documents to the authorities more than once. of course, these services are also accessible to people with disabilities. however, for the digitisation of public services to be successful, we must not forget the key prerequisite for its functioning: a fast and stable internet connection. this must be accessible to all, including people living in rural or sparsely populated areas, such as vulnerable households. the commission will continue to monitor the implementation of the union's policy on digital services, in particular by ensuring that the union's digital services are not misused. only then can we harness the full potential of this process.
