the rapporteur. madam president, madam commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would first of all like to thank the rapporteur, mr sokol, the shadow rapporteurs in the chamber and all the staff of the negotiating team for the work they have done on this matter: the commission has already decided to take a decision on the proposal for a directive on the approximation of the laws of the member states relating to the use of the electricity sector in the electricity sector. i would like to try to make a small political premise, especially for those who are most skeptical of this instrument. our text represents a significant improvement on the european commission proposal, which differs not in proportion but in the value we have given and recognised to personal health data, which have been deemed to deserve the highest level of privacy, especially with regard to so-called 'secondary uses', which do not concern the main purpose of patient care, if not indirectly. after listening to the representatives of the categories concerned, i am convinced that the european health data area can be a useful tool to provide better patient health care and cutting-edge health care in the european union and in all our countries, especially those countries that have not yet fully experienced the digitisation of health data, such as italy. all the work has been done from the outset in my capacity as rapporteur for the libe committee, aimed at introducing strong guarantees for respect for citizens' privacy, a priority and a must for us, taking into account also the experience gained in countries where electronic health records have been a reality for years. in these countries, it is possible to avoid repeated diagnostic tests, even invasive ones, to travel in skirts and cds for medical advice to a region or a state other than your own or for a second opinion of a health professional. in emergency situations, medical personnel can access the electronic file even if the patient is unconscious, check for any allergies, pre-existing treatments or diseases, and save a person's life. we have worked hard to achieve this goal, while also keeping in mind the need for our data to be available to everyone. we have therefore requested the application of rigorously tested techniques to ensure the confidentiality of information contained in electronic health data when processed, thus making it anonymous. we have also introduced the so-called opt-out system which, contrary to what the european commission has proposed, will allow citizens to choose to exclude all or part of their health data from the system for secondary uses. secondary uses not directly related to patient care but which are nonetheless important, such as research on life-saving medicines. the text provides that, under strict conditions, researchers and companies can access anonymised health data in a secure processing environment where strict technical and safety standards are applied. the amendments we have made to the parliamentary examination allow the citizen, unlike the text proposed by the european commission, to legitimately choose to use the system only for health care, and therefore for his own care, excluding further use. all you have to do is make an informed and conscious choice. however, i would like to stress the main point: we'll have a clinical record accessible via a smartphone or pc, we'll be able to decide who to allow access to, what data to hide or not share with some professionals, and we'll be able to check the latest accesses. it will be possible to decide which doctor to allow access to your data and which not, also because of the doctor's specialization. we have also included some provisions for those who are less tech savvy, because they may be older, but they will still have to have full control over their health data. in general, i believe that the work done in these months has enabled us to grasp the main challenge of the dossier, namely to strike the right balance between the use and exchange of health data for medical care purposes on the one hand, and the protection of personal data on the other. it wasn't easy, and you won't find us... (the president-in-office takes the floor from the speaker)

in relation to the decisions of certain committees to open interinstitutional negotiations under article 71 (1) of the regulation, announced at the opening of the sitting on monday 11 december, the president has not received any request for a vote in the chamber from a number of members or one or more political groups equal to at least the average threshold. the committees can therefore enter into negotiations.
let's move on to the discussions. the agenda shall contain the statements by the council and the commission in preparation for the meeting of the european council of 14 and 15 december 2023 (2023/2937(rsp)).
president-in-office of the council. the european council will meet in brussels on the december european council, following a summit with the leaders of the western balkans, which will be held this afternoon. the agenda of this european council will be tight and, as always, the meeting will begin with a change of views with president metsola. they and the leaders will again address the issue of russia's war of aggression against ukraine. they will also discuss the extension and the draft revision of the multiannual financial framework. security and defence will also be on the agenda, as will the latest developments in the middle east. ukraine remains one of the highest priorities for the european council. the country is facing its second winter of war and it is important that we reaffirm our unwavering determination to continue to provide our financial, economic, humanitarian, military and diplomatic support for as long as it is needed. the leaders will take stock of the latest developments on the ground. they will also address ukraine's urgent military and defence needs and stress the importance of continuing and sustainable military support to ukraine, in particular through the european peace support fund, the european union military assistance mission and the direct bilateral assistance of member states. they are also likely to highlight the urgent need to speed up the delivery of missiles and ammunition to ukraine and to supply more air defense systems. following the high representative's report, the european council will discuss the union's future security commitments to ukraine. secondly, based on the talks held in granada and the recent enlargement report of the commission, as well as on the discussions at yesterday's general affairs council, the european council will hold a comprehensive enlargement debate. both the future member states and the union must be prepared for the prospect of further enlargement of the union. yesterday, at the general affairs council, we adopted conclusions on enlargement. leaders will provide guidance on the next steps for the aspiring countries, ratify the conclusions adopted yesterday and finally make the most relevant policy decisions on the enlargement process. they will also address the european union's capacity to absorb new member states and the reforms needed to do so. thirdly, the situation in the middle east will once again call for the full attention of the leaders. they will not only review the latest developments but also look ahead to relaunch a political process based on a two-state solution. fourth, the european council will re-examine the proposal for a review of the 2021-2027 multiannual financial framework following the first debate in october. following the intensive work undertaken since then, the leaders will address how the union's main political priorities are financed. the current presidency of the council is confident that an agreement will be reached at this next european council. the european council will resume its debate in june on security and defence, another item on the agenda. and in a context of global instability, strategic competition and security threats, we must continue to strengthen our defence, investment and capacity readiness. the european council will continue to debate the adoption of new decisive measures necessary to make the union's defence stronger and more capable. the council will then continue to deal with migration, which remains an urgent concern. it is expected that the leaders will re-examine this issue and discuss the state of affairs within the institutions, within the council. finally, a number of other issues are on the agenda. first, they will address the issue of hybrid attacks launched by russia and belarus at the union's external borders and the instrumentalisation of migration. next, as you all know, the council will also discuss post-cop28 action in the current climate emergency context. in view of the worrying incidents that have taken place in europe, it is expected that the leaders will condemn all forms of hatred, intolerance, anti-semitism, racism and xenophobia, including anti-muslim hatred, and the european council will also address the eu's relations with turkey. finally, following the informal meeting in granada and taking into account the union's long-term objectives, the european council will consider the preparations for the new strategic agenda, which will set priorities and set the course for the next institutional cycle. these are the issues of the european council.
president of the commission. – madam president, minister, honourable members, i want to focus this morning on two main issues. one is the enlargement topic and the other one the mid-term review. we should remember that exactly two years ago, right here in strasbourg, we discussed for the first time russia’s military build-up at ukraine’s border – two years ago now. and these have been two painful years. we all remember every moment of them. we remember the siege of kyiv, the people taking refuge in subway stations, the maternity ward in mariupol, the body bags in bucha. we remember all the pain that putin has inflicted on innocent ukrainians, as well as on europeans with its energy blackmail. but besides the pain, we must also remember the light that has shone through these dark days, the courage of the ukrainian resistance, the joy of those who were liberated from russian occupation, the solidarity of an entire continent standing at ukraine’s side. none of this seemed plausible or even possible two years ago. so let me, first of all, thank all of those who made it happen. above all, the amazing people of ukraine, but also europe that stood tall and united. ukraine has resisted also because of the people of europe who have opened their hearts and their homes, because of the member states’ financial and military aid, because of this parliament’s unwavering support. you have empowered ukraine’s resistance and you have kept hope alive. now, a new winter of struggle approaches for ukraine. we have a duty to be clear-eyed about what we have achieved so far and what lies ahead. when russia invaded ukraine, many feared that kyiv would fall within days. that did not happen. instead, ukraine has driven russia out of half of the territories it has captured. at sea, ukraine has pushed back russia’s fleet and reopened the maritime corridor to deliver grain to the world – i think this is completely underreported. in the air, ukraine has become incredibly effective at shooting down russian missiles and drones. and on land, ukraine is imposing massive losses on russia. tens of thousands of young russian soldiers get killed or wounded every week. and further away from the battlefield, finland has become a nato member. sweden soon will be. ukraine is on its way to eu membership. the kremlin has deprived itself from western economies and innovation systems and made itself dependent on china. so putin is not only missing his strategic goals, he is also imposing a dramatic cost on his own country. but we all know that putin’s failure will not automatically translate into ukraine’s victory. as the war drags on, we must prove what it means to support ukraine for as long as it takes. ukraine is not only fighting against the invader, but for europe, and joining our family will be ukraine’s ultimate victory. and for this we have a decisive role to play. ukraine is making great strides to pass the reforms that will lead to our union. our enlargement report from last month showed clear progress on all steps that we had identified. over 90% of those reforms had been completed at that time. we identified four reforms to fully complete all steps. and in this month between the enlargement report and now, ukraine has continued to work on all of them. the rada has just approved two anti-corruption laws, on the staff of the national anti-corruption bureau and assets declaration, as well as an amended law on national minorities to address the remaining recommendation of the venice commission. this law will allow greater use of minority languages in schools, in books, and in public events. actually, national minority groups have already reacted positively to this law, and our initial assessment is also positive. if effectively implemented, these actions can fulfil three of our four outstanding recommendations. ukraine’s government has also proposed a new law on lobbying to curb the power of oligarchs and address our last recommendation. honourable members, this is hard work and the goal of fulfilling all seven steps is within reach. i think ukraine is showing us by this hard work and the speed, how much they care about our union and our values, and we should match their determination. honourable members, besides our political support, ukraine also needs our sustained financial support. and this will also be discussed, of course, in the next two days at the european council. when we shaped the current eu budget in 2020, no one could predict an all-out war on european soil. its shockwaves have spread well beyond ukraine. the union budget had to deal with an energy crisis, with a global food crisis, with the arrival of millions of ukrainians fleeing russia’s bombs and all of this against a backdrop of rising prices and interest rates – because, of course, russia’s war had also added to the inflationary pressures of a global economy that was just recovering from the pandemic. and now the deadliest war in decades has erupted in the middle east, calling for greater european support not only in gaza but across the region. none of this could be predicted when the mff was created. none of this can be solved by simply shifting money within the budget. and this is why we have proposed a revision of our budget. we have proposed a package of four major updates to the mff. the first is that we want to guarantee stable and substantial financing to ukraine over the next four years. that will provide confidence of the investors and of course, it will provide hope to the ukrainian fighters. we must give ukraine what it needs to be strong today so that it can be stronger tomorrow at the table when it is negotiating a long-lasting and just peace for ukraine. the second element is on migration. you all know that in these four years we have raised our ambition on anti-smuggling, on managing our external borders, on partnerships with third countries. we have designed the pact for migration and asylum and an agreement has never been so close. now, greater ambition on migration must be backed by appropriate funding on the european level. the third element is competitiveness. all member states want to boost the cleantech industries of the future, rightly so. we should support them by making better use of the available investments we have with simplified rules and speedy procedures, because we all know that the future of these strategic industries must be in europe. and the fourth point: we also need some technical adjustments to the budget. the union’s budget, like other national budgets too, is feeling the pressure of inflation and higher interest rates. this is, of course, a consequence of war and pandemic and we have to factor this in. now, what i see in all the preparations for the european council and the discussion that we have: there is a growing consensus on the priorities. of course, the question is how to finance them. and we know that national public finances are under pressure. we will all face difficult choices and we must leave no stone unturned. and this is why, more than ever, the union must be able to finance its priorities with its own resources. this has been a long-standing plea of this parliament, a plea that i fully support. thanks to the parliament, back in 2020, we agreed on a roadmap to put in place new revenues for the union budget. we need to make this happen now. the next long-term budget is just around the corner, and europe must have the financial firepower to deliver on the historic tasks of this decade and of the next one. so, honourable members, this will be the main topics discussed at the european council, i thank you for your attention, and long live europe!

on behalf of the id group. the family is one of the foundations of western civilization. it is the first link in our social organisation and ensures the necessary development and protection of children. this is why i have long advocated the preservation and strengthening of a policy of support for families. but this can only be done at national level, as each state has its own conception of the family. the report aims to europeanise family protection by creating a european parenthood certificate. this is an interference, all the more serious in national jurisdiction, which will require each member state to recognise different parentage established outside its borders and thus impose the recognition of pregnancy for others throughout europe. the report admits this in a hardly veiled way, striving to remove the exception provided for in private international law, which allows each state to refuse to recognise a membership incompatible with its public order. i am, however, very much against other people's pregnancy, which is nothing more than a new form of exploitation of the bodies of women in poor countries by couples in rich countries. respect for the dignity of women requires that this commercialisation of their reproductive function be avoided. for these two fundamental reasons preservation of a conception of the family and hostility to the gpa , i will therefore vote against the european parental certificate.
mr karas, commissioner reynders, rapporteur maria leitão, and by this regulation which creates a single european certificate of membership on a legal basis, which is a mandate of the treaty on the functioning of the european union, article 81, mutual recognition of legal documents in civil matters, and which serves to realize a fundamental right, not only the free movement of all persons without discrimination, but also the best interests of the child, enshrined as such in articles 24 and 32 of the charter of fundamental rights of the european union. the child's best interests which must be protected and regulated throughout the european union, regardless of the family background. insofar as a person has all his or her fundamental rights at his or her disposal, he or she must have the right not only to free movement, but also to education, maintenance and succession on an equal footing with any other person, regardless of the family in which he or she is enrolled. no child can therefore be held responsible for their origin. whether or not he is legally admitted to a legal system, he is a person with all his fundamental rights and therefore also the right to freedom of movement with a single european certificate of membership.
mr president, commissioner reynders, rapporteur leitão-marques, the regulation we are discussing today aims to guarantee, once and for all, the full legal recognition of parenthood throughout the european union. with the regulation, parenthood that is recognised in one member state, automatically will be recognised by all, fully and mandatorily. by doing so, we put children first and we ensure the rights of all kinds of family, regardless of their sex, country of origin or country of residence. this will prevent any member state from backtracking and hindering progress and rights. a victory in one member state shall be a victory in all. this text also contributes to harmonising the norms of international private law in the area of parenthood, strengthening the rights and protection of children when they move within our union. we are providing legal security to minors and reducing litigation in the cases of divorce of parents that reside in different member states. ultimately, we are advancing on european rights and freedoms, bringing our entire union to the 21st century and protecting european children.
